class="ltx_contact ltx_role_email"><span class="ltx_contact_name">email: </span><simpeter@cs.washington.edu> </span></span></span></span>

</div>

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract.

We present Presto, the first TCP stack that delivers ASIC-class performance and energy efficiency on programmable Reconfigurable Match-Action Table (RMT) pipelines, providing flexibility while retaining standard TCP semantics and POSIX socket compatibility. The key challenge in designing Presto is reconciling TCP’s complex, dependent state updates with RMT’s unidirectional, lock-step execution model. To overcome this challenge, Presto introduces three novel techniques: optimistic concurrency (speculative updates validated downstream), pseudo-segment injection (circular dependency resolution without stalls), and bump-in-the-wire processing (single-pass segment handling). Together, these enable TCP retransmission, reassembly, flow, and congestion control, as a pipeline of simple match-action operations.

Our Intel Tofino 2 prototype demonstrates Presto’s scalability to terabit speeds, flexibility, and robustness to network dynamics. Presto matches RDMA performance and efficiency for both RPC and streaming workloads (including NVMe-oF with SPDK), while maintaining TCP/POSIX compatibility. Presto saves up to 16 host CPU cores versus state-of-the-art kernel-bypass TCP, while achieving 5$`\times`$ lower 99.99p tail latency and 2$`\times`$ better throughput-per-watt for key-value stores. At scale, Presto drives nearly $`1`$ Bpps at 20 $`\mu`$s RPC tail latency. Unlike fixed-function offloads, Presto supports transport evolution through in-data-path extensions (selective ACKs, congestion control variants, application co-design for shared logs). Finally, Presto generalizes to FPGA SmartNICs, outperforming Tonic’s monolithic design by $`3\times`$ under equal timing.

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1. </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

In modern datacenters, the “CPU tax” of networking has become unsustainable. Even with state-of-the-art kernel-bypass TCP stacks, applications spend up to 48% of per-packet CPU cycles in the stack (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>). Yet, software TCP stacks remain the default due to their broad compatibility, flexibility, and robust semantics. As networks scale to terabits and latency targets tighten, operators face an increasingly untenable trade-off among performance, efficiency, and flexibility. CPU-based stacks cannot keep pace, yielding poor bandwidth utilization, high energy consumption, and excessive overhead. At the other extreme, ASIC transports, such as TCP offload engines (TOEs) and remote direct memory access (RDMA), are highly efficient, but inflexible and operationally brittle in the cloud (<a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref">Singhvi et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">Skiadopoulos et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib99" class="ltx_ref">Singhvi et al., 2025</a>), and are thus largely confined to high-performance computing and storage deployments (<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">Bai et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib88" class="ltx_ref">Gangidi et al., 2024</a>). Programmable network processing units (NPUs) (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>) and FPGAs (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">Arashloo et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">Lim et al., 2024</a>) only partially bridge this gap and still face limitations in scalability, complexity, and energy efficiency.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

We break this tension by leveraging the Reconfigurable Match-Action Table (RMT) architecture (<a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref">Bosshart et al., 2013</a>), widely deployed in programmable network switches (<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">Corporation, 2023</a>) and SmartNICs (<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref">Pensando, 2024</a>). RMT provides deterministic, energy-efficient line-rate packet processing through a sequence of match-action stages, while retaining a programmable execution model that has enabled a wide range of networking tasks (<a href="#bib.bib68" class="ltx_ref">Song et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib73" class="ltx_ref">Sharma et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#bib.bib74" class="ltx_ref">Sharma et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#bib.bib75" class="ltx_ref">Goyal et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">Li et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib78" class="ltx_ref">Lee et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref">Wang et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib80" class="ltx_ref">Kim et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">Kim et al., 2020</a>). Realizing TCP in RMT, however, is challenging. TCP requires complex, interdependent state updates, consistent state for bidirectional flow coordination, and buffering for segment reassembly and retransmission, all ill-suited to RMT’s strictly unidirectional pipeline with limited local state and tight timing constraints. Bridging this mismatch would unlock ASIC performance and efficiency with software flexibility.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

We present Presto, the first TCP stack tailored to the RMT architecture. Presto reimagines TCP as a pipeline of lightweight match-action operations, enabling line-rate processing without buffering and delivering practical TCP at terabit speeds while preserving high throughput, low latency, energy efficiency, flexibility, and interoperability. To reconcile TCP’s complex state dependencies and RMT’s unidirectional flow, Presto introduces: <span id="S1.I1" class="ltx_inline-enumerate"> <span id="S1.I1.i1" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(1)</span> *optimistic concurrency with deferred validation*<span id="S1.I1.i1.3" class="ltx_text">to resolve *forward read dependencies*, where earlier stages depend on state available only in later stages, by performing speculative updates validated downstream; </span></span> <span id="S1.I1.i2" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(2)</span> *pseudo segment injection*<span id="S1.I1.i2.3" class="ltx_text">, which eliminates *circular write dependencies* without side-effects or pipeline stalls; and </span></span> <span id="S1.I1.i3" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(3)</span> <span id="S1.I1.i3.2" class="ltx_text">a *bump-in-the-wire* design that avoids recirculation and performs stateful TCP segment processing in a single pipeline pass. </span></span> </span> Together, these principles enable terabit-scale performance, while maintaining TCP semantics and POSIX socket compatibility.

</div>

<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S1.F1.9" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>. </span><span id="S1.F1.10" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;"> Challenges in mapping TCP reassembly onto an RMT pipeline. <span id="S1.F1.10.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(a)</span> CPU: the stack freely reads and updates state multiple times. <span id="S1.F1.10.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(b)</span> RMT: placing state in different stages introduces a <em>forward read dependency</em>. <span id="S1.F1.10.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(c)</span> RMT: Deferring updates to resolve this dependency creates a <em>circular write dependency</em>. <span id="S1.F1.10.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(d)</span> RMT: Serializing updates stalls the pipeline. </span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

We make the following contributions:

</div>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S1.I2.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I2.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I2.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">A match-action TCP design.</span> We present Presto’s design principles, showing how to map TCP’s dependency-heavy data-path onto a unidirectional, match-action pipeline with tight timing and resource constraints. While rooted in TCP, these principles apply broadly across stateful protocols and programmable network platforms, including SmartNICs, FPGAs, and hybrid systems that combine programmable and fixed-function components.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I2.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I2.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I2.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Prototype on multiple hardware targets.</span> We demonstrate that these principles are realizable on real hardware with a Presto design and implementation on an Intel Tofino 2 switch (<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">Corporation, 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib87" class="ltx_ref">Netberg, 2024</a>), showing for the first time that RMT can support a TCP stack with ASIC-like performance and energy efficiency while retaining programmability. We establish generalizability with our Presto port to an FPGA SmartNIC; under identical timing constraints it supports $`3\times`$ higher packet rates than Tonic (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">Arashloo et al., 2020</a>) and orders of magnitude higher than Beehive (<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">Lim et al., 2024</a>), while consuming significantly fewer hardware resources. Our code is available at <a href="https://github.com/tcp-acceleration-service/Presto" class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter">https://github.com/tcp-acceleration-service/Presto</a>.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I2.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I2.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I2.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Performance, robustness, flexibility, and interoperability.</span> We compare Presto to Linux, TAS (<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Kaufmann et al., 2019</a>), and RDMA (§<a href="#S5" class="ltx_ref" title="5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>). Presto surpasses TAS’s peak throughput using 16 fewer CPU cores and matches RDMA performance and efficiency for both RPCs and streaming workloads (up to $`25`$Mpps per core, enough to saturate $`1.6`$Tbps with $`8`$K MTU). At scale, Presto drives nearly $`1`$Bpps with RPC tail latency near $`20\mu s`$ and remains resilient to packet loss and congestion (up to $`2\times`$ higher throughput than TAS under loss), while interoperating with other TCP stacks. Presto benefits real applications: it improves a key-value store’s throughput-per-watt by $`2\times`$ while maintaining 99.99p tail latency below TAS’s best case, and enables NVMe-over-TCP to achieve RDMA-level performance and CPU efficiency. We demonstrate flexibility with transport extensions (Timely (<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">Mittal et al., 2015</a>) congestion control, delayed and selective ACKs) and application optimizations, including an in-network sequencer for a shared log, similar to NoPaxos (<a href="#bib.bib92" class="ltx_ref">Li et al., 2016</a>).

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2. </span>Background

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Can we map TCP’s stateful, dependency-heavy data path onto a lock-step match-action pipeline at line-rate? To approach this question, we first summarize the RMT programming model that underlies high-speed programmable switches and SmartNICs (§<a href="#S2.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="2.1. Match-Action Architecture ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.1</span></a>). We then explain why TCP poses significant challenges for this model (§<a href="#S2.SS2" class="ltx_ref" title="2.2. Challenges for TCP on RMT ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.2</span></a>), as TCP’s per-segment processing creates forward read dependencies (Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>b), circular write dependencies (Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>c), and, if we avoid both by serializing, pipeline stalls (Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>d).

</div>

<div id="S2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.1. </span>Match-Action Architecture

<div id="S2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The reconfigurable match-action table (RMT) architecture targets line-rate packet processing. It exploits packet-level parallelism and a fixed-latency pipeline of stages that apply match-action logic to a per-packet header vector (PHV). Modern implementations sustain billions of packets per second with sub-microsecond latency and low ASIC power consumption (<a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref">Bosshart et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#bib.bib87" class="ltx_ref">Netberg, 2024</a>).

</div>

<div id="S2.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Pipeline execution.

<div id="S2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Packets enter through a MAC/DMA channel and are parsed into a PHV. The PHV then traverses a sequence of stages that execute in lock-step. Within each stage, simple ALU operations update PHV fields, accessing *stage-local* state via tightly-timed memory operations. Because stages are synchronized, packet processing latency is fixed by the number of occupied pipeline stages. Implementations typically split processing into ingress and egress pipelines, with a traffic manager (TM) in between. The TM buffers bursts and schedules packets to match output bandwidth; it can also participate in lossless operation via backpressure (e.g., PFC).

</div>

<div id="S2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

RMT provides limited ways to feed information back into the pipeline: <span id="S2.I1" class="ltx_inline-enumerate"> <span id="S2.I1.i1" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(i)</span> <span id="S2.I1.i1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">recirculation</span><span id="S2.I1.i1.3" class="ltx_text">(send a packet for another full pass), </span></span> <span id="S2.I1.i2" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(ii)</span> <span id="S2.I1.i2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">mirroring</span><span id="S2.I1.i2.3" class="ltx_text">(duplicate PHV and reinject), and </span></span> <span id="S2.I1.i3" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(iii)</span> <span id="S2.I1.i3.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">packet generator</span><span id="S2.I1.i3.3" class="ltx_text">(emit packets on triggers). </span></span> </span> These mechanisms are useful but must be used sparingly: recirculation doubles latency and consumes pipeline bandwidth; mirroring is cheaper but still consumes scheduling and pipeline resources. Finally, uncommon processing and configuration are handled by a CPU control plane that can directly read and write pipeline state, and exchange packets via a dedicated DMA channel.

</div>

<figure id="S2.F2" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 2</span>. </span><span id="S2.F2.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Presto RMT data-path functional blocks.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.2. </span>Challenges for TCP on RMT

<div id="S2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

TCP’s reliable in-order semantics couple each segment’s processing to shared per-connection state (e.g., window boundaries and out-of-order bookkeeping). On a CPU, the stack can read and update this state multiple times in a single run-to-completion handler (Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>a). On an RMT pipeline, however, state is stage-local and the PHV flows only forward, so even simple TCP logic quickly runs into architectural constraints.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Prior work, such as TAS (<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Kaufmann et al., 2019</a>) and FlexTOE (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>), splits TCP into a fast data-path and a slower control path, but assumes a programmable execution model with run-to-completion processing and flexible memory access per packet (e.g., a CPU or NPU). In contrast, RMT executes a fixed-depth, lock-step match-action pipeline: packets carry a bounded amount of metadata and cannot revisit earlier stages, while per-flow state is accessed only through stage-local stateful operations. As a result, the primary challenge on RMT is not the fast/slow decomposition, but managing TCP’s state dependencies without feedback, buffering, or stalls.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

In particular, placing related TCP state—or, more generally, any reliable transport (§<a href="#S7" class="ltx_ref" title="7. Discussion ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>)—in different stages forces one of three problematic mappings: <span id="S2.I2" class="ltx_inline-enumerate"> <span id="S2.I2.i1" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(i)</span> <span id="S2.I2.i1.2" class="ltx_text">if an early stage must update state based on a value stored later, the design introduces a *forward read dependency*, which cannot be resolved (Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>b); </span></span> <span id="S2.I2.i2" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(ii)</span> <span id="S2.I2.i2.2" class="ltx_text">if we defer the update until the later value is known, the update must effectively flow backward to earlier-stage state. This creates a *circular write dependency* that requires loopback via recirculation or mirroring (Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>c), which consumes bandwidth, adds latency, and can transiently expose stale state; and </span></span> <span id="S2.I2.i3" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(iii)</span> <span id="S2.I2.i3.2" class="ltx_text">serializing updates to avoid both (e.g., freezing state and committing later) stalls the pipeline and squanders parallelism (Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>d).</span></span> </span>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3. </span>Presto Overview

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">

To overcome the challenges of §<a href="#S2.SS2" class="ltx_ref" title="2.2. Challenges for TCP on RMT ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.2</span></a>, Presto rearchitects TCP’s per-connection transport logic to maximize RMT pipeline parallelism. We adopt these design principles, each addressing a specific RMT constraint:

</div>

<div id="S3.p2" class="ltx_para">

1.  <span id="S3.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">1.</span></span>
    <div id="S3.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

    <span id="S3.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Bump-in-the-wire processing (avoid stalls):</span> Presto processes each TCP segment at most once in the pipeline (no recirculation on the common path) and never buffers segments in the pipeline, preserving both bandwidth and low latency under tight memory constraints.

    </div>
2.  <span id="S3.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">2.</span></span>
    <div id="S3.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

    <span id="S3.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Optimistic segment processing (handle forward reads):</span> To deal with forward read dependencies, Presto executes the common case speculatively (in-window segment receipt and delivery) and defers validation to later stages; uncommon cases fall back to corrective actions.

    </div>
3.  <span id="S3.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">3.</span></span>
    <div id="S3.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

    <span id="S3.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Pseudo segment cyclic updates (resolve circular writes):</span> To resolve circular write dependencies without stalling the pipeline, Presto injects *pseudo segments* via mirroring. These segments are processed like regular TCP segments, but are crafted to trigger the required updates to earlier-stage state without application-visible side effects.

    </div>

</div>

<div id="S3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Like many other proposals (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Kaufmann et al., 2019</a>), Presto accelerates TCP processing for established connections—the common case for high-performance applications—and divides the TCP stack into three key components: <span id="S3.I2" class="ltx_inline-enumerate"> <span id="S3.I2.i1" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(i)</span> <span id="S3.I2.i1.2" class="ltx_text">A *control plane* orchestrates connection lifecycle and data-path resources, and executes congestion control policies. It may run either on the host or on a control CPU alongside the RMT pipeline. </span></span> <span id="S3.I2.i2" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(ii)</span> <span id="S3.I2.i2.2" class="ltx_text">An *application interface* enables direct interaction with the RMT data-path, exposing both a POSIX-compatible sockets API (libPresto) and a zero-copy API (libPresto-ZC) to applications. libPresto-ZC allows data-intensive applications to access libPresto receive and transmit buffers directly, avoiding unnecessary data copies, while libPresto provides a conventional socket API that copies user buffers into libPresto receive and transmit buffers. </span></span> <span id="S3.I2.i3" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(iii)</span> <span id="S3.I2.i3.2" class="ltx_text">The *RMT data-path* executes core TCP transport logic to ensure reliable delivery of ordered byte streams directly to/from libPresto.</span></span> </span>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Data-path architecture.

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

For established connections, Presto executes TCP transport logic as a single RMT pipeline triggered by *events* from three sources: the network MAC (incoming TCP segments), the host DMA interface (application payload and notifications), and periodic/explicit triggers (window/credit refresh). These events induce three logical workflows: RX (network $`\rightarrow`$ host delivery), TX (host payload $`\rightarrow`$ network transmission), and SYNC (connection state update). Although we describe them separately, they all traverse the same match-action pipeline and touch shared per-connection state, reflecting TCP’s bidirectional semantics.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

To achieve extensibility, Presto structures this pipeline as a sequence of decoupled, independently programmable functional blocks (Figure <a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ Pipeline execution. ‣ 2.1. Match-Action Architecture ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). Each block spans multiple match-action stages and is subject to the state and dependency constraints of the RMT model. The *ingress* portion performs stateless classification and preparation: <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMi5waWMxLjEiPjE8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Classification identifies whether the event corresponds to RX, TX, or SYNC and selects the corresponding fast path; <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.pic2" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMi5waWMyLjEiPjI8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Mux/Demux retrieves connection and context identifiers; <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.pic3" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMi5waWMzLjEiPjM8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Scheduler triggers segment transmissions subject to per-flow rate limits; and <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p2.pic4" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMi5waWM0LjEiPjQ8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Header Transformation constructs the required protocol and DMA headers before handing the packet/metadata to the traffic manager (TM) for egress processing.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3" class="ltx_para">

The *egress* portion executes the stateful transport logic for all three workflows. For RX, <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMy5waWMxLjEiPjU8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Receive Window validates sequence state, detects gaps/loss signals, and records out-of-order segments, while <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.pic2" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMy5waWMyLjEiPjY8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Transmit Window handles acknowledgment processing and loss detection; <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.pic3" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMy5waWMzLjEiPjc8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Data Placement translates sequence numbers to host buffer addresses for DMA; and <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:14.0pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.pic4" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxyZWN0IGZpbGw9Im5vbmUiIGhlaWdodD0iMCIgcng9IjAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjQiIHdpZHRoPSIwIiB4PSIwIiB5PSIwIiAvPjwvZz48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDApIj48cmVjdCBmaWxsPSJub25lIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjAiIHJ4PSIwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC40IiB3aWR0aD0iMCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCIgLz48L2c+PGcgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJ0cmFuc2xhdGUoMCwwKSI+PGcgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJzY2FsZSgxLC0xKSI+PHRleHQgY29sb3I9IiNGRkZGRkYiIHhtbDppZD0iUzMuU1MwLlNTUzAuUHgxLnAzLnBpYzQuMSI+MTA8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Application Notification informs the host of newly arrived data. For TX, <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.pic5" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMy5waWM1LjEiPjY8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Transmit Window enforces flow control, while <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.pic6" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMy5waWM2LjEiPjk8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Rate Control enforces credit-based transmission rates. For SYNC, the same window and rate control blocks apply credit updates. Finally, <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p3.pic7" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMC5TU1MwLlB4MS5wMy5waWM3LjEiPjg8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> Protocol & Congestion Signaling collects congestion/ACK state and prepares acknowledgments that are emitted without involving the host.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p4" class="ltx_para">

Crucially, Presto restricts *mutable* protocol state updates to the lossless egress pipeline, treating ingress as strictly read-only. This makes Presto <span id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">drop resistant</span>: TM drops are equivalent to network loss and naturally recovered by TCP retransmission, preserving correctness.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p5" class="ltx_para">

We now describe a baseline instantiation of these blocks that implements a complete, standards-compliant TCP stack that integrates directly with real-world datacenter applications running on the host. We focus on segment reassembly (§<a href="#S3.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1</span></a>) and (re-)transmission (§<a href="#S3.SS2" class="ltx_ref" title="3.2. (Re-)transmission ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>), as they concentrate the hardest RMT-mapping challenges and largely determine correctness and throughput. In §<a href="#S5.SS3" class="ltx_ref" title="5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.3</span></a>, we evaluate how the same blocks can be reused and extended to support additional transport extensions and application-specific optimizations.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1. </span>Segment Reassembly

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Segment reassembly involves incorporating any relevant part of an RX segment within the receive window, delivering in-order data to the application, buffering out-of-order (OOO) segments for future delivery, collecting congestion metrics, generating acknowledgments, and notifying libPresto of newly available data.

</div>

<figure id="S3.F3" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S3.F3.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 3</span>. </span><span id="S3.F3.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">RMT TCP segment reassembly paths in <span id="S3.F3.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:9.8pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.F3.pic2" class="ltx_picture"><img src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLkYzLnBpYzIuMSI+NTwvdGV4dD48L2c+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4=" /></span></span>.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.1.1. </span>Receive Window Tracking (Figure <a href="#S3.F3" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 3 ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>)

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Dynamic data structures are infeasible in RMT, so Presto implements reassembly using a *fixed* amount of per-connection state. We define the design’s reassembly fidelity by the number of out-of-order (OOO) intervals—contiguous sequence ranges received ahead of the cumulative acknowledgment point—that it can track simultaneously. This yields a spectrum from OOO-0 (drop all OOO segments, no tracking state) to OOO-$`n`$ (tracking $`n`$ OOO intervals). Section <a href="#S5.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="5.4. Robustness ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.4</span></a> quantifies this fidelity-performance trade-off, while Table <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 2 ‣ Application co-design ‣ 5.3.1. Case Studies ‣ 5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> shows the resource costs.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Baseline reassembly (OOO-0).

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

OOO-0 maintains only two state variables: <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> (the next expected sequence number) and <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span> (remaining receive-window space). For an in-order segment, the receiver must <span id="S3.I3" class="ltx_inline-enumerate"> <span id="S3.I3.i1" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(i)</span> <span id="S3.I3.i1.2" class="ltx_text">validate that the segment fits in the advertised window and </span></span> <span id="S3.I3.i2" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(ii)</span> <span id="S3.I3.i2.2" class="ltx_text">advance <span id="S3.I3.i2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> and decrement <span id="S3.I3.i2.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span> by the accepted payload length. </span></span> </span> Mapping this directly to an RMT pipeline creates a *forward read dependency*: the stage that advances <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> needs the current <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span> value to validate the window.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Presto resolves this dependency using *optimistic execution*. Well-behaved senders respect the advertised flow-control window, so Presto speculatively assumes the segment is in-window and validates that assumption later. Concretely, in <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p2.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MxLlB4MS5wMi5waWMxLjEiPjE8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span>, Presto trims/drops duplicates and *optimistically* advances <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> for in-order segments, forwarding a snapshot of the updated <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> to later stages. In <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p2.pic2" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MxLlB4MS5wMi5waWMyLjEiPjI8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span>, it then decrements <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span> by the (trimmed) accepted length and performs the definitive out-of-window (OOW) check using the now-updated window state.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p3" class="ltx_para">

If the segment (partially) exceeds the advertised receive window (a sender protocol violation), the speculative update in <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p3.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MxLlB4MS5wMy5waWMxLjEiPjE8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> has advanced the receive state incorrectly. Presto detects this in <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p3.pic2" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MxLlB4MS5wMy5waWMyLjEiPjI8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span>, drops the segment, and raises an exception to the control plane, which restores the per-connection state to its previous values. During this brief recovery window, <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span> may become negative due to the speculative decrement. While <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px1.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span>\<0, subsequent segments fail boundary checks and are dropped, and Presto advertises a zero receive window to pause the sender until the restoration completes. Because the receiver accepts payload only after final validation, externally visible behavior stays correct.

</div>

<figure id="S3.F4" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S3.F4.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 4</span>. </span><span id="S3.F4.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">TCP reassembly state (OOO-1) in sequence space.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Robust reassembly (OOO-1).

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

OOO-1 improves robustness by tracking a single OOO “island” non-contiguous with <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span>. It adds two per-connection state variables, <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail</span> and <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-head</span>, stored as *offsets relative to <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p1.1.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span>* (Figure <a href="#S3.F4" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 4 ‣ Baseline reassembly (OOO-0). ‣ 3.1.1. Receive Window Tracking (Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>). This representation keeps the OOO interval aligned as the receive window advances. An <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail</span>=0 indicates that no OOO interval is currently tracked.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

For in-order segments, as <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> advances, Presto decrements <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail</span> and <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-head</span> by the same accepted length, preserving the island’s absolute position. For out-of-order segments, if the segment overlaps with (or is adjacent to) the tracked interval, Presto merges it by updating the interval boundaries. If no interval is active (<span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail</span>=0 in <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p2.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MxLlB4Mi5wMi5waWMxLjEiPjM8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span>), the arriving OOO segment initializes <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail</span> and <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-head</span>. Segments that do not overlap the single tracked range are dropped.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p3" class="ltx_para">

A difficult case arises when an in-order segment closes the gap to the tracked OOO interval, making the byte stream contiguous through the end of that interval (Figure <a href="#S3.F5" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 5 ‣ Robust reassembly (OOO-1). ‣ 3.1.1. Receive Window Tracking (Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>). At this point the receiver should advance the window to the end of the OOO island and free the corresponding space, i.e., update <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> and <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span>. However, this condition is only known once <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-head</span> is inspected (<span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p3.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MxLlB4Mi5wMy5waWMxLjEiPjQ8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span>), while <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> and <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p3.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span> reside in earlier stages—a *circular write dependency*.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p4" class="ltx_para">

Presto resolves this without stalling the pipeline using a two-step merge. When <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p4.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MxLlB4Mi5wNC5waWMxLjEiPjQ8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> detects that the gap is closed (i.e., <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-head</span> reaches 0), it injects a *pseudo-segment*. The pseudo-segment carries no payload but encodes a sequence range spanning the newly contiguous bytes (from the current <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p4.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> through <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p4.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail</span>). When processed, it appears as a standard in-order segment, so the OOO-0 logic advances <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p4.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> and decrements <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p4.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span> and the OOO interval is cleared.

</div>

<figure id="S3.F5" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2504.19058v4/tcp-reassembly-ooo-merge.png" id="S3.F5.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:476/136;" width="476" height="136" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S3.F5.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 5</span>. </span><span id="S3.F5.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Resolving <em>circular write dependency</em> using pseudo-segments (OOO-1).</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p5" class="ltx_para">

Intuitively, pseudo-segments allow Presto to express backward state updates using the same forward execution path as real packets, preserving pipeline invariants without introducing special cases or stalls. Pseudo-segments preserve correctness during the transient period before the merge commits. If intervening in-order segments advance <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> first, the pseudo-segment’s overlapping prefix is naturally trimmed in <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p5.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MxLlB4Mi5wNS5waWMxLjEiPjE8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span>, preventing double-accounting. A pseudo-segment merge also cannot create an OOW condition: the OOO interval was only formed from segments that were previously validated against the receive window in <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p5.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px2.p5.pic2" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MxLlB4Mi5wNS5waWMyLjEiPjI8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span>. Pseudo-segments are best-effort; if one is dropped under congestion, the OOO interval remains tracked and the next triggering segment re-injects it, yielding eventual consistency.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### OOO-$`n`$.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The same approach extends to tracking multiple OOO intervals by adding <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail-2</span>, <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-head-2</span>, etc. across subsequent stages. To keep processing simple (and avoid sorting primitives), Presto maintains two invariants: <span id="S3.I4" class="ltx_inline-enumerate"> <span id="S3.I4.i1" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(i)</span> <span id="S3.I4.i1.2" class="ltx_text">active intervals are stored in increasing sequence order (i.e., <span id="S3.I4.i1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail-$`i`$</span> $`\geq`$ <span id="S3.I4.i1.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail-$`(i-1)`$</span>). </span></span> <span id="S3.I4.i2" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(ii)</span> <span id="S3.I4.i2.2" class="ltx_text">There are no “holes”. If <span id="S3.I4.i2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail-$`i`$</span>=0 then <span id="S3.I4.i2.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail-$`(i+1)`$</span>=0. </span></span> </span> With these invariants, merging is a single pass: an arriving OOO segment is tested against each active interval and merged on overlaps. Otherwise, it cascades to the first inactive slot and initializes a new interval.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px3.p2" class="ltx_para">

When an in-order segment closes the gap from <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">next-seq</span> to one or more intervals, Presto again uses pseudo-segments to commit the resulting window advance. The exceptional case is when closing OOO-1 would leave a hole before an active OOO-2, violating the second invariant. Presto handles this with a cascading merge: in the triggering pass, it snapshots and clears the subsequent active intervals (Figure <a href="#S3.F6" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 6 ‣ OOO-𝑛. ‣ 3.1.1. Receive Window Tracking (Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>). To do so, it treats the in-order segment as having an effective length greater than the maximum window size for the purposes of processing subsequent OOO intervals. Since in-order OOO maintenance simply decrements <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-tail-i</span> and <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px3.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ooo-head-i</span> by the segment length (as in OOO-1), this deterministically clears all downstream active intervals with no new match-action logic. Presto then injects one pseudo-segment per cleared interval that includes the snapshots. These pseudo-segments are replayed in order, advancing the window or re-initializing intervals without leaving holes.

</div>

<figure id="S3.F6" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S3.F6.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 6</span>. </span><span id="S3.F6.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Cascading merge of multiple out-of-order intervals.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Replenishment.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

As the receiver delivers data, it must replenish window space. libPresto triggers the SYNC workflow after the application consumes more than a configurable fraction of its receive buffer (default: $`\frac{1}{4}`$). On SYNC, the Receive Window block increments <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.Px4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">avail</span> by the amount supplied by libPresto. Because SYNC events traverse the same lossless egress pipeline as RX, replenishment serializes naturally with segment processing, ensuring consistent updates.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.1.2. </span>Data Placement (<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MyLnBpYzEuMSI+NzwvdGV4dD48L2c+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4=)</span></span> in Figure <a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ Pipeline execution. ‣ 2.1. Match-Action Architecture ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>)

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Leveraging our *bump-in-the-wire* design principle, Presto directly places received payload into host memory via DMA, even for OOO segments. Presto maintains a per-connection host receive buffer in libPresto, and the Data Placement block translates TCP sequence numbers into offsets within this buffer to resolve host memory addresses for DMA transfers.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

To handle buffer wraparound, Presto double-buffers: the receive buffer is mapped twice consecutively in libPresto’s virtual address space, sharing the same physical memory. This lets a single contiguous DMA span the logical end of the buffer rather than splitting into multiple transfers.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.1.3. </span>Acknowledgment Generation (<span id="S3.SS1.SSS3.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS3.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMS5TU1MzLnBpYzEuMSI+ODwvdGV4dD48L2c+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4=)</span></span> in Figure <a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ Pipeline execution. ‣ 2.1. Match-Action Architecture ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>)

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Acknowledgment generation in Presto occurs after segment reassembly. The Protocol & Congestion Signaling block governs acknowledgment generation based on post-reassembly window snapshots. When an ACK is required, Presto generates a pseudo-segment encapsulating the reassembly state; this segment is mirrored and transformed into a valid TCP acknowledgment. If the pipeline drops the pseudo segment, the effect is identical to network packet loss of a TCP acknowledgment. Mirroring acknowledgments does not increase pipeline workload, as it replaces host-generated ACK packets rather than introducing additional traffic. As the TX workflow also traverses the reassembly path, it can generate piggybacked acknowledgments on outgoing segments based on receive state. Presto conserves pipeline bandwidth by coalescing pseudo-segments; for instance, the OOO-1 merge operation (§<a href="#S3.SS1.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="3.1.1. Receive Window Tracking (Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1.1</span></a>) piggybacks on the corresponding ACK pseudo-segment.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS4" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.1.4. </span>Application Notification (<span id="S3.SS1.SSS4.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:14.0pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS1.SSS4.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxyZWN0IGZpbGw9Im5vbmUiIGhlaWdodD0iMCIgcng9IjAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjQiIHdpZHRoPSIwIiB4PSIwIiB5PSIwIiAvPjwvZz48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDApIj48cmVjdCBmaWxsPSJub25lIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjAiIHJ4PSIwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC40IiB3aWR0aD0iMCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCIgLz48L2c+PGcgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJ0cmFuc2xhdGUoMCwwKSI+PGcgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJzY2FsZSgxLC0xKSI+PHRleHQgY29sb3I9IiNGRkZGRkYiIHhtbDppZD0iUzMuU1MxLlNTUzQucGljMS4xIj4xMDwvdGV4dD48L2c+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4=)</span></span> in Figure <a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ Pipeline execution. ‣ 2.1. Match-Action Architecture ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>)

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

The Application Notification block informs libPresto when newly received data becomes available for application consumption, by including a notification inline with the DMA data transfer. For each <span id="S3.SS1.SSS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">in-order</span> segment that advances the receive window, Presto notifies libPresto of the highest contiguous buffer offset that is ready to be consumed by the application.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

When a received segment closes the gap to an existing out-of-order interval (§<a href="#S3.SS1.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="3.1.1. Receive Window Tracking (Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1.1</span></a>), Presto issues the notification immediately, ahead of the subsequent pseudo-segment merge, to minimize notification latency.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2. </span>(Re-)transmission

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

(Re-)transmission in Presto regulates the sending rate, selects data for transmission, and recovers from packet loss, driven by data-path transport state.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Presto adopts a <span id="S3.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">push-based</span> transmission model optimized for low latency on uncongested flows, which dominate datacenter workloads (<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">Kalia et al., 2019</a>). The data-path grants transmission credits to libPresto via SYNC messages; upon receiving credits, libPresto proactively pushes eligible payloads into the data-path using DMA writes, allowing immediate transmission aligning with the *bump-in-the-wire* design principle. By contrast, <span id="S3.SS2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">pull-based</span> scheduling adds a roundtrip interaction between libPresto and the data-path, raising latency. The same design supports a pull-based variant by transforming SYNCs into DMA reads; we leave this to future work.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.2.1. </span>Congestion Control

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

To remain robust to packet loss and congestion, Presto supports DCTCP (<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">Alizadeh et al., 2010</a>) by default. Like other TCP stacks (<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Kaufmann et al., 2019</a>; <a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#bib.bib96" class="ltx_ref">Narayan et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#bib.bib98" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2025</a>; <a href="#bib.bib99" class="ltx_ref">Singhvi et al., 2025</a>), Presto locates the congestion control policy in the control plane for extensibility. The data-path collects per-connection metrics (acknowledged bytes, ECN-marked bytes, duplicate ACKs) in the Protocol & Congestion Signaling block; the control plane periodically collects these metrics and updates data-path tables with computed transmission rates.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS1.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Scheduling via SYNC

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS1.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Based on the configured rate, the data-path issues periodic SYNC messages that convey transmission credits to libPresto. SYNCs are generated directly in the RMT pipeline using the packet generator, without additional control-plane involvement. The Scheduler block (<span id="S3.SS2.SSS1.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS2.SSS1.Px1.p1.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMi5TU1MxLlB4MS5wMS5waWMxLjEiPjM8L3RleHQ+PC9nPjwvZz48L2c+PC9zdmc+)</span></span> in Figure <a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ Pipeline execution. ‣ 2.1. Match-Action Architecture ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>) assigns SYNCs to flows according to the chosen scheduling policy and rate, controlling both SYNC frequency and the credits granted per SYNC. SYNCs also signal retransmission timeouts; in this case, libPresto resets its transmission head and initiates retransmissions.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS1.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

To be work conserving, Presto pauses SYNC workflows for inactive connections (no data transmission for $`N`$ RTTs). For uncongested flows with short messages that underutilize credits, the control plane reduces SYNC frequency. libPresto may also self-pace SYNCs for such flows, triggering them only as the transmit window nears exhaustion.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.2.2. </span>Transmission

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

libPresto pushes new data from the host transmit buffer into the data-path via DMA when sufficient credits are available. TCP sequence numbers are derived from DMA offset within the transmit buffer. The Transmit Window block tracks transmit state using logic analogous to the receive side (§<a href="#S3.SS1.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="3.1.1. Receive Window Tracking (Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1.1</span></a>): segments are validated against the current transmit window. Segments below the cumulative acknowledgment point are dropped and segments extending beyond the window advance it accordingly (<span id="S3.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS2.SSS2.p1.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMi5TU1MyLnAxLnBpYzEuMSI+MzwvdGV4dD48L2c+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4=)</span></span>). Transmission is credit-limited and flow-controlled in the Rate Control block (<span id="S3.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS2.SSS2.p1.pic2" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMi5TU1MyLnAxLnBpYzIuMSI+OTwvdGV4dD48L2c+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4=)</span></span>), ensuring the sending rate respects both allocated credits and the peer’s advertised receive window.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.2.3. </span>Acknowledgment Processing

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

For received ACKs, the Transmit Window block (<span id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:10.4pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1.pic1" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSJub25lIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxjaXJjbGUgZmlsbD0ibm9uZSIgcj0iMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCI+PC9jaXJjbGU+PC9nPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ic2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjx0ZXh0IGNvbG9yPSIjRkZGRkZGIiB4bWw6aWQ9IlMzLlNTMi5TU1MzLnAxLnBpYzEuMSI+NjwvdGV4dD48L2c+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4=)</span></span> in Figure <a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ Pipeline execution. ‣ 2.1. Match-Action Architecture ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>) updates transmit window state, including the cumulative acknowledgment point, merging any SACK blocks to track lost segments, mirroring the logic of the receive side (§<a href="#S3.SS1.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="3.1.1. Receive Window Tracking (Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1.1</span></a>). When sufficient transmit window space becomes available, the Application Notification block (<span id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_align_center ltx_inline-block" style="width:14.0pt;position:relative; bottom:-2.0pt;"><span id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1.pic2" class="ltx_picture">![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyB4bWxucz0iaHR0cDovL3d3dy53My5vcmcvMjAwMC9zdmciIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgd2lkdGg9IjEwMC4wMCIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuMDAiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMC4wMCkgc2NhbGUoMSwtMSkiPjxnIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsMCkiPjxyZWN0IGZpbGw9Im5vbmUiIGhlaWdodD0iMCIgcng9IjAiIHN0cm9rZT0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlLXdpZHRoPSIwLjQiIHdpZHRoPSIwIiB4PSIwIiB5PSIwIiAvPjwvZz48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDApIj48cmVjdCBmaWxsPSJub25lIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjAiIHJ4PSIwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC40IiB3aWR0aD0iMCIgeD0iMCIgeT0iMCIgLz48L2c+PGcgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJ0cmFuc2xhdGUoMCwwKSI+PGcgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJzY2FsZSgxLC0xKSI+PHRleHQgY29sb3I9IiNGRkZGRkYiIHhtbDppZD0iUzMuU1MyLlNTUzMucDEucGljMi4xIj4xMDwvdGV4dD48L2c+PC9nPjwvZz48L3N2Zz4=)</span></span>) transforms the ACK into a notification to libPresto to reclaim buffer space.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS3.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Fast Retransmission

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS3.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">
