Upon three duplicate ACKs, Presto triggers fast retransmission and notifies libPresto of the updated transmit window state (including SACK blocks) via DMA. The Rate Control block simultaneously reduces transmission credits, similar to TCP Reno, and conveys the updated credits to libPresto in the same DMA notification.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4. </span>Implementation

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Presto’s data-path is implemented on an Intel Tofino 2-based switch, a commercially available RMT pipeline architecture. The system comprises 20,775 lines of code (LoC) across the data-path, control-plane, and libPresto. The P4 code for the offloaded data-path has 2,597 LoC. The control plane is split between the host and a switch-resident CPU (<span id="S4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SwitchConf</span>), which configures the match-action tables, executes congestion control policy, and processes control notifications. <span id="S4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SwitchConf</span> interacts with the data-path via Intel’s proprietary bfrt C++ API. They are implemented in 3,352 and 6,917 C++ LoC respectively. libPresto is implemented in 7,909 C++ LoC. For DMA, libPresto utilizes RDMA (<span id="S4.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">libibverbs</span>) without kernel intervention. The host control-plane and POSIX emulation in libPresto are adapted from TAS. Our prototype leverages some RDMA and Tofino 2 features for optimizations, described below.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### DMA

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

libPresto interacts with the data-path via RDMA unreliable connection (UC) queue pairs, one per application context (thread), akin to per-core NIC queues, with each context managing multiple connections. Data transfers use RDMA Writes (with immediate for notifications) directly to/from per-connection payload buffers. libPresto allocates per-connection payload buffers and registers them with the RDMA NIC, storing the buffer’s virtual address and remote memory key in the data-path. Based on available transmission credits, libPresto can issue RDMA writes larger than the MTU, relying on the NIC’s segmentation offload to dynamically packetize the data, and paces transmissions over the SYNC interval to smooth bursts. Note that we use RDMA solely for DMA—reliability, ordering, and congestion control are all handled by the data-path. For flow control, PFC is enabled between the host and the switch. External-facing switch ports connected to hosts outside the subnet using TCP do not require PFC. For Presto implementations on RMT-based SmartNICs with PCIe-based DMA interfaces, PFC is unnecessary for lossless operation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Interrupt-driven operation

<div id="S4.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

By default, libPresto polls the completion queue for events. After 10ms of inactivity, it switches to interrupt-mode by via RDMA completion event channels, letting application sleep while waiting for IO. Periodic SYNC notifications from the data-path are also paused for inactive connections. This reduces the excessive CPU overhead and energy consumption due to polling.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1. </span>Testbed Constraints

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Below are some constraints of our testbed, which are specific to the Tofino 2 architecture and can be elided in non-switch platforms.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Presto-Presto connections

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Same-switch Presto-Presto connections require recirculation because both server and client stacks reside on one switch; non-switch platforms avoid this limitation. All TCP traffic for such connections, segments and ACKs alike, must recirculate to the data-path. For example, an RPC request leaves libPresto as an RDMA write, the switch data-path converts it to a TCP segment, recirculates it for peer reassembly, and converts it back to an RDMA write. The RPC response and ACKs also undergo recirculation. Recirculation adds latency ($`\approx 750ns`$) and is limited by $`3\times 100`$G recirculation port bandwidth, with connections sharded across the three ports.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Single-pipeline operation

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Although the Tofino 2 features multiple pipelines, our prototype confines data-path processing to a single pipeline. Because we maintain all mutable state within the egress pipeline to ensure drop resistance (§<a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref" title="3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>), the sender and receiver must share the same pipeline, precluding cross-pipeline connections. The remaining pipelines can operate independently as isolated subnets.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Checksums

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our prototype omits payload checksums (including TCP and RDMA iCRC), as the Tofino 2 checksum engine lacks support; a production version would include hardware checksum units. Because the RDMA iCRC is appended after the payload, our prototype requires the sender to add Ethernet padding for the iCRC in TCP segments.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5. </span>Evaluation

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate Presto by addressing the following questions:

- <span id="S5.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Performance:</span> Can Presto deliver predictable $`\mu`$s latency and high message rate for RPCs (§<a href="#S5.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.1</span></a>), sustain terabit throughput with near-zero CPU utilization (§<a href="#S5.SS2" class="ltx_ref" title="5.2. Byte Streaming ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.2</span></a>), scale efficiently to thousands of applications and connections (§<a href="#S5.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.1</span></a>), and benefit real-world storage (§<a href="#S5.SS2.SSS2" class="ltx_ref" title="5.2.2. NVMe-over-TCP ‣ 5.2. Byte Streaming ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.2.2</span></a>) and key-value stores (§<a href="#S5.SS1.SSS3" class="ltx_ref" title="5.1.3. Energy Efficiency ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.1.3</span></a>)?

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Efficiency:</span> Does Presto improve performance-per-watt over host-based stacks (§<a href="#S5.SS1.SSS3" class="ltx_ref" title="5.1.3. Energy Efficiency ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.1.3</span></a>)?

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Flexibility:</span> Can Presto customize transport logic for critical datacenter applications (§<a href="#S5.SS3" class="ltx_ref" title="5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.3</span></a>)?

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Robustness:</span> Is Presto resilient to loss and congestion, and does it ensure performance isolation between latency-sensitive and bandwidth-intensive workloads (§<a href="#S5.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="5.4. Robustness ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.4</span></a>)?

  </div>
- <span id="S5.I1.i5"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S5.I1.i5.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S5.I1.i5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Generalizability:</span> Do Presto’s pipelined transport logic principles extend to FPGA-based SmartNICs (§<a href="#S5.SS5" class="ltx_ref" title="5.5. Generalizability ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.5</span></a>)?

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Testbed

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our evaluation cluster comprises eight Intel Xeon Gold 6430 machines, each with 32-core (64 hyperthreads) running at 2GHz, 256GB RAM, and 126.5MB aggregate cache. Each machine is equipped with a RDMA-capable 200G Mellanox ConnectX-6 NIC, all connected to an 32$`\times`$<!-- -->400G Intel Tofino-2 based Netberg Aurora 810 switch. We configure the NICs in 100G mode because the switch fails to negotiate with the NICs at 200G speeds. Two machines are designated as servers, with Priority Flow Control (PFC) enabled for lossless RDMA, while the remaining machines function as clients. To ensure high performance and low latency, we disable turbo boost and set frequency scaling to performance mode. Power measurements are obtained via the IPMI interface of both the machines and the switch. <span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SwitchConf</span> runs on a 4-core (8 hyperthreads) onboard Intel Xeon D-2123IT CPU, and its power consumption is included in the switch power measurements.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Baselines

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We compare Presto performance against the Linux TCP stack, the TAS kernel-bypass TCP stack, and RDMA. Presto interoperates with both TCP baselines (cf. §<a href="#S4.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="4.1. Testbed Constraints ‣ 4. Implementation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.1</span></a>). Unless otherwise specified, clients use the TAS network stack to generate network traffic. TAS struggles with large flows due to high payload copy overheads, failing to saturate the 100G line rate with a single connection. To mitigate this bottleneck in such client workloads, we elide payload copying in TAS and transmit junk data instead (TAS-nocopy). Across all TCP-based baselines, we use identical application binaries built on a sockets-based <span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">epoll()</span> interface; for the RDMA baseline, we adapt the benchmarks to use the <span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">libibverbs</span> interface (a separate verbs-based code path), whereas Presto uses the same sockets-based applications via its POSIX interposition layer. We confirm real-world application compatibility through FlexKVS (§<a href="#S5.SS1.SSS3" class="ltx_ref" title="5.1.3. Energy Efficiency ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.1.3</span></a>) and SPDK NVMe-oF (§<a href="#S5.SS2.SSS2" class="ltx_ref" title="5.2.2. NVMe-over-TCP ‣ 5.2. Byte Streaming ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.2.2</span></a>); more broadly, our POSIX emulation layer is <span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">epoll()</span> and <span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">libevent</span> compatible, and runs unmodified datacenter applications, including *memcached* (<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">memcached, 2020</a>), *nginx* (<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">Sysoev, 2026</a>), and NVMe-oF clients (cf. (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">Stolet et al., 2023</a>), §<a href="#S5.SS2.SSS2" class="ltx_ref" title="5.2.2. NVMe-over-TCP ‣ 5.2. Byte Streaming ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.2.2</span></a>). Unless otherwise noted, our evaluation configures Presto with OOO-1 reassembly and a simple OOO-0 (go-back-N) sender recovery as a baseline. We also implemented OOO-1 SACK, which enables selective recovery using the same OOO interval abstraction, but compilation issues in the Intel Tofino 2 toolchain prevent hardware evaluation. We therefore evaluate OOO-1 SACK in simulation (Section <a href="#S5.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="5.4. Robustness ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.4</span></a>). Importantly, go-back-N is not fundamental to Presto’s pipeline architecture: it is a baseline choice that offers the best performance-complexity trade-off, and Presto supports higher-fidelity reassembly and selective recovery mechanisms (cf. §<a href="#S5.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="5.4. Robustness ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.4</span></a>, Table <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 2 ‣ Application co-design ‣ 5.3.1. Case Studies ‣ 5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). We use DCTCP as the default congestion control policy.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.1. </span>Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs)

<div id="S5.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

RPC workloads demand low latency, high packet rates, and scalability across many applications and connections, requirements that heavily tax host TCP stacks. Presto’s bump-in-the-wire design efficiently delivers constant, low TCP processing latency even at multi-million segment rates, fully eliminating all CPU overhead. We demonstrate the benefits with an echo server benchmark.

</div>

<figure id="S5.F7" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F7.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 7</span>. </span><span id="S5.F7.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Load-latency across network stacks. </span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">5.1.1. </span>Load-latency

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S5.F7" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 7 ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> presents the round-trip latency profile of a 32-core echo server across stacks, as load increases. The benchmark scales connections across multiple clients, each leaving a single 64B RPC in-flight. Presto’s performance closely matches RDMA, sustaining nearly $`40`$ MOps/sec ($`1.67\times`$ TAS) with an $`18\mu s`$/$`24\mu s`$ median/99.99p tail latency. Recirculation in Presto-Presto connections adds $`\sim 2\mu s`$ relative to RDMA (§<a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref" title="4. Implementation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>). Our setup bypasses egress processing for non-Presto packets, reducing baseline RTTs; a conventional switch would require both ingress and egress. Both Presto and RDMA experience 99.99p tail latency spikes as they approach the NIC’s processing limits. A hybrid setup (Presto server with TAS clients) achieves a similar peak throughput but with slightly higher latency due to client-side TAS overheads. Linux is especially inefficient, delivering $`10\times`$ lower throughput and $`78\times`$ higher latency than Presto. Presto’s sockets interface adds a copy between libPresto and user buffers; libPresto-ZC eliminates it, reaching RDMA parity.

</div>

<figure id="S5.F8" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S5.F8.fig1" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S5.F8.fig2" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F8.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 8</span>. </span><span id="S5.F8.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Core and connection scalability.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">5.1.2. </span>Scalability

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate scalability along cores, connections, and pipeline capacity. We repeat the previous experiment sweeping across host, core, and connection counts, finding the throughput-latency knee for each configuration.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS2.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Cores

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS2.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S5.F8" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 8 ‣ 5.1.1. Load-latency ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> shows that Presto scales linearly with server cores. Data-path performance is independent of core count, as Presto multiplexes connections onto per-core DMA channels (<span id="S5.SS1.SSS2.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">contexts</span>), steering payload directly to application cores (like aRFS (<a href="#bib.bib59" class="ltx_ref">Documentation, 2025b</a>)) and avoiding RDMA scalability limits. Performance peaks at 8 cores, where Presto hits the NIC line rate, surpassing TAS’s peak throughput while keeping tail latency $`4.3\times`$ lower. Linux remains far behind.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS2.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Connections

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS2.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S5.F8" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 8 ‣ 5.1.1. Load-latency ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> showcases Presto’s superior connection scalability for a single host. Presto saturates NIC bandwidth at 768 connections, achieving $`1.8\times`$ TAS and $`10\times`$ Linux throughput. Beyond NIC line rate, additional connections slightly reduce throughput. Accessing connection state via stage-local memory at constant latency, Presto sustains 32K connections without performance loss. Sharding TCP state tables across stages or multiple pipelines supports higher connection counts (§<a href="#S5.SS3" class="ltx_ref" title="5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.3</span></a>). Other high-performance stacks degrade with many connections due to cache pressure (<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">Kalia et al., 2019</a>) or limited on-NIC memory (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>).

</div>

<figure id="S5.F9" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S5.F9.fig1" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S5.F9.fig2" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F9.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 9</span>. </span><span id="S5.F9.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Pipeline packet processing scalability.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS2.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Pipeline

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS2.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Each RMT pipeline in our testbed is capable of processing $`1.2`$ Bpps across $`8\times 400`$G links. Using 8 hosts running Presto, exchanging 8B echo RPCs, stresses the pipeline. Each RPC generates 8 packets through the egress pipeline—4 for the request (TX, RX, ACK generation, ACK processing) and 4 for the response. Figure <a href="#S5.F9" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 9 ‣ Connections ‣ 5.1.2. Scalability ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">9</span></a> highlights pipeline scalability. Latency stays flat and narrow up to $`850`$ Mpps ($`71\%`$ utilization), after which recirculation bandwidth saturates and tail latency rises. Two more TAS clients (black dots) push throughput near $`1`$ Bpps ($`80\%`$ utilization), with aggregate pipeline throughput nearing $`500`$ Gbps ($`130`$ MOps/sec goodput) even at 8B payloads. Latency is higher in this configuration due to client-side TAS overheads. We have tested scalability up to 8K concurrent connections and 256 contexts in this benchmark. Notably, Presto does not experience ACK pseudo-segment drops, even under such extreme loads; the mirroring engine reliably sustains the required packet rate ($`\approx 250`$ Mpps). Because OOO pseudo-segments are piggybacked on ACKs (§<a href="#S3.SS1.SSS3" class="ltx_ref" title="3.1.3. Acknowledgment Generation ( 8 in Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1.3</span></a>), Presto ensures robust OOO reassembly and recovery without imposing additional load on the pipeline.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">5.1.3. </span>Energy Efficiency

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We measure the power consumption of the TCP stacks using FlexKVS (<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">Kaufmann et al., 2016</a>), a scalable, high-performance key-value store inspired by memcached. Short RPCs dominate these workloads, with host TCP stacks consuming up to 48% of per-request CPU cycles (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>). Our benchmark uses 1M uniformly distributed key-value pairs (8B keys, 64B values, $`0.99`$ GET:SET ratio). Baseline power is first recorded for the server (including NIC) and switch. The workload then runs for 5 minutes while measuring total power and application performance. We vary server threads and connections, with each connection limited to a single in-flight request.

</div>

<figure id="S5.F10" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S5.F10.fig1" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S5.F10.fig2" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F10.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 10</span>. </span><span id="S5.F10.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">(a)<span id="S5.F10.6.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Throughput-per-watt and tail latency for FlexKVS key-value store. </span>(b)<span id="S5.F10.6.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_medium"> Pipeline dynamic power consumption with utilization.</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S5.F10" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 10 ‣ 5.1.3. Energy Efficiency ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">10</span></a>a shows throughput-per-watt and tail latency across server configurations (# threads, \# connections). Presto consistently beats host stacks: even its worst case exceeds TAS’s best by $`1.2\times`$ in throughput-per-watt at $`5\times`$ lower 99.99p latency; its best configuration doubles TAS’s throughput-per-watt and beats TAS’s best tail latency. Linux fares far worse, with at least an order of magnitude lower efficiency and two orders higher latency. Switch power varies by no more than 4W between baselines and Presto, with efficiency driven primarily by reduced host CPU usage. RMT-based switches consume power comparable to non-programmable commodity switches of similar bandwidth (<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">Agrawal and Kim, 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref">Bosshart et al., 2013</a>), in contrast to power-hungry FPGAs (<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref">AMD, 2026a</a>).

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.SSS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

Finally, Figure <a href="#S5.F10" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 10 ‣ 5.1.3. Energy Efficiency ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">10</span></a>b breaks down dynamic power (increase from idle, measured over 10 minutes) as a function of pipeline throughput, showing Presto processes TCP at $`\approx 25`$ Mpps/W for 8B payload packets. These switch measurements include the on-board CPU, RMT pipeline, transceivers, and other fixed-function components (cf. Table <a href="#S5.T1" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 1 ‣ Coverage of the design surface ‣ 5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>).

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.2. </span>Byte Streaming

<div id="S5.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Streaming bandwidth is critical for modern storage and AI workloads. CPU-based stacks like Linux and TAS fail to saturate line rate on a single core, even with hardware-assisted payload copy optimizations (<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Cai et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>). Specialized DMA copy engines (<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">Skiadopoulos et al., 2024</a>) do not resolve this, as single-core protocol processing is the bottleneck (§<a href="#S6" class="ltx_ref" title="6. Related Work ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>). Consequently, hardware transports like RDMA and Chelsio TOE, despite their inflexibility, are increasingly preferred for streaming (<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref">Bai et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref">Gao et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">Chelsio Communications, 2016</a>; <a href="#bib.bib32" class="ltx_ref">Qian et al., 2024</a>). Can Presto match their performance?

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">5.2.1. </span>Single-core Throughput

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this benchmark, a client running TAS-nocopy transmits packets in open-loop 1MB streaming bursts (similar to iperf (<a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref">ESnet, 2023</a>)) to a single server core that discards incoming payloads. To provide different intensities of protocol to payload processing, the benchmark varies the segment size by adjusting the maximum transmission unit (MTU). To avoid payload copying in the sockets layer, we use the libPresto-ZC interface, comparing against the equivalent low-level TAS interface (TAS-ll), though TAS still requires copying the payload between network buffers and application memory.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S5.F11" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 11 ‣ 5.2.1. Single-core Throughput ‣ 5.2. Byte Streaming ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">11</span></a> shows that Presto matches RDMA’s single-core packet rate of ~25 Mpps, independent of packet size, reaching line rate for higher MTUs, sufficient to exceed 1.6 Tbps ($`=25M\times 8\times 8K=1600G`$) with 8K MTU. The bottleneck is NIC and CPU processing (<a href="#bib.bib95" class="ltx_ref">Kalia et al., 2016a</a>), not the RMT pipeline, whose capacity is far greater (cf. Figure <a href="#S5.F9" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 9 ‣ Connections ‣ 5.1.2. Scalability ‣ 5.1. Remote Procedure Calls (RPCs) ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">9</span></a>). Even a single connection can exploit the pipeline’s full parallelism and bandwidth. TAS is limited to ~5 Mpps per core—a bottleneck shared by NPU-based stacks (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>), dropping further above 512 B due to copy overhead. Linux, again, performs poorly.

</div>

<figure id="S5.F11" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F11.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 11</span>. </span><span id="S5.F11.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Streaming performance across segment sizes.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">5.2.2. </span>NVMe-over-TCP

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Modern datacenters are rapidly adopting large-scale disaggregated storage. We integrate SPDK (<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">Foundation, 2024</a>), an open-source user-space storage stack, with Presto to show that NVMe-oTCP can rival RDMA in both performance and efficiency, while retaining a TCP/POSIX programming model.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

We extend SPDK with a new transport that interfaces with Presto via libPresto-ZC, delivering NVMe commands and payloads into per-connection buffers directly accessible by SPDK, avoiding intermediate copies and enabling high throughput. A key challenge in supporting NVMe-oTCP is that NVMe devices exploit parallelism by allowing commands to complete out-of-order (OOO). To support this, we provide a modified version of libPresto-ZC that exposes explicit mechanisms to free connection buffer space out-of-order while tracking the buffer head. NVMe-oRDMA provides similar functionality by requiring SPDK to post multiple receive buffers to the RDMA work queue, which are freed independently upon command completion.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate our NVMe-oTCP storage target using 4KB random writes. In our setup with a single NVMe SSD, Presto can easily exceed the 1M IOPS disk bandwidth, even when only partially utilizing a server core. To shift the bottleneck from the device to the CPU, we configure a RAM block device (<a href="#bib.bib101" class="ltx_ref">SPDK, 2024a</a>), which emulates Non-Volatile Memory (NVM) or Compute eXpress Link (CXL)-attached memory.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S5.F12" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 12 ‣ Takeaway ‣ 5.2.2. NVMe-over-TCP ‣ 5.2. Byte Streaming ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">12</span></a> compares the performance of a single-core SPDK NVMe-oF target using the Linux kernel stack, the Presto transport, and RDMA, as IO-depth increases. We use the SPDK perf (<a href="#bib.bib102" class="ltx_ref">SPDK, 2024b</a>) tool on a remote client node<span id="footnote1" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>1</sup> <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span> SPDK perf can generate more load compared to fio (<a href="#bib.bib102" class="ltx_ref">SPDK, 2024b</a>).</span></span></span> that drives the load through Presto’s POSIX sockets interposition layer. We use an MTU of 4,400 with Linux, 4,096 with RDMA and Presto.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS2.p5" class="ltx_para">

Presto closely matches RDMA in throughput and tail latency until the server core saturates at high IO depths from storage-stack processing and RAM block device copies. Presto’s peak throughput is slightly lower from periodic SYNC grant overhead, and its latency slightly higher due to client-side sockets layer copies. Linux, by contrast, incurs extra copies and higher stack overhead, leaving fewer application cycles and $`>4\times`$ lower throughput.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS2.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Takeaway

<div id="S5.SS2.SSS2.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Matching RDMA in this end-to-end storage setting shows that TCP semantics need not imply CPU-bound transport processing: Presto achieves RDMA-class efficiency while preserving TCP/POSIX compatibility and leaving room for transport evolution in the data-path.

</div>

<figure id="S5.F12" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F12.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 12</span>. </span><span id="S5.F12.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">SPDK NVMe-oF performance: 4K Random Writes.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.3. </span>Flexibility

<div id="S5.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Unlike fixed-function hardware transports, Presto provides programmable transport functionality within the constraints of RMT pipelines, enabling customization both within and beyond standard TCP features, stack design, and application integration. We evaluate flexibility systematically: we first quantify baseline resource usage and then measure the incremental cost of extensions along key design dimensions.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Resource footprint

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We begin by quantifying the resource footprint of our baseline design. As shown in Table <a href="#S5.T1" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 1 ‣ Coverage of the design surface ‣ 5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, it consumes a fraction of available pipeline resources while supporting 32K connections and 1K contexts. Presto’s data-path sustains 3.2 Tbps ($`8\times 400`$G) line-rate or 1.2 Bpps at a total processing latency (excluding queuing delays) of 395ns and a power consumption of 2.81W for the RMT pipeline (excluding SerDes, MAC, TM). With basic L2/L3 forwarding implemented, the low footprint leaves ample headroom for new functions. For manageability, Presto exports telemetry and debugging metrics for internal transport state via P4 counters—OOO segments, retransmissions, congestion events, and pseudo-segments.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Coverage of the design surface

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Transport evolution is broad, but most changes fall into a small number of axes: <span id="S5.I2" class="ltx_inline-enumerate"> <span id="S5.I2.i1" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(i)</span> <span id="S5.I2.i1.2" class="ltx_text">transport semantics (e.g., ACK and reassembly strategy, loss recovery hints), </span></span> <span id="S5.I2.i2" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(ii)</span> <span id="S5.I2.i2.2" class="ltx_text">congestion control hooks (which metrics are measured, and how they drive pacing/scheduling), and </span></span> <span id="S5.I2.i3" class="ltx_inline-item"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_inline-item">(iii)</span> <span id="S5.I2.i3.2" class="ltx_text">application integration (what events are exposed and how data is placed/copied). </span></span> </span> Presto’s modular blocks expose these axes directly, so extensions typically require localized changes rather than redesigning the pipeline. Conversely, Presto does not aim to support unbounded per-flow data structures or complex control-flow in the data-path; such functionality is delegated to the control plane or endpoints.

</div>

<figure id="S5.T1" class="ltx_table">

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">5.3.1. </span>Case Studies

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Presto’s architecture is modular (Figure <a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ Pipeline execution. ‣ 2.1. Match-Action Architecture ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>), with each block exposing a distinct dimension of programmability. These dimensions include: transport semantics (reassembly and ACK strategies), congestion control (policy, metrics, and scheduling), and application integration (notifications, APIs, and data placement). We next illustrate how these flexibility dimensions can be exercised in practice through multiple extensions and reassembly strategies, each time incurring a modest resource cost (Table <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 2 ‣ Application co-design ‣ 5.3.1. Case Studies ‣ 5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>).

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Reassembly fidelity

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our reassembly fidelity spectrum (§<a href="#S3.SS1.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="3.1.1. Receive Window Tracking (Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1.1</span></a>) enables operators to trade-off resource usage for robustness by tuning the Receive Window block. As shown in Table <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 2 ‣ Application co-design ‣ 5.3.1. Case Studies ‣ 5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>, OOO-$`n`$ reassembly variants fit within Tofino 2 constraints; each increment in fidelity requires two additional stages and results in a modest power increase.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Transport extensions

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We modify the Protocol & Congestion Signaling block to support alternative acknowledgment strategies, including delayed and selective ACK variants.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Delayed or cumulative ACKs (<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">Braden, 1989</a>) acknowledge every $`M`$ consecutively received segments, reducing sender and network load (<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">Alizadeh et al., 2010</a>). For instance, for RPCs, piggybacking the ACK onto the response can halve sender overhead. In Presto, a counter tracks unacknowledged segments, issuing an ACK pseudo-segment at threshold $`M`$ (or immediately for OOO segments); transmitted segments reset it. The control plane manages the delayed ACK timer. Benchmarking with a Presto echo-server and TAS client (single fast-path core) shows delayed ACKs improve peak throughput by 24% by lowering client CPU load.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px2.p3" class="ltx_para">

Selective ACKs (<a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">Floyd et al., 1996</a>) enable targeted retransmissions of lost segments, accelerating recovery in high-loss scenarios. We implement 1-OOO selective ACKs by extending the ACK generation logic to encode the 1-OOO interval, as discussed in §<a href="#S3.SS1.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="3.1.1. Receive Window Tracking (Figure ) ‣ 3.1. Segment Reassembly ‣ 3. Presto Overview ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1.1</span></a>, and verify that the design fits within Tofino 2 constraints (cf. §<a href="#S5.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="5.4. Robustness ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.4</span></a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Congestion control

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Congestion control remains an evolving research area with numerous proposals addressing diverse network conditions and performance objectives (<a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref">Singhvi et al., 2020</a>). Timely (<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">Mittal et al., 2015</a>), which infers congestion from round-trip time (RTT) variations, is a notable example. Recent RMT hardware enhancements (<a href="#bib.bib82" class="ltx_ref">Yuan et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">Corporation, 2023</a>), including support for lightweight arithmetic and look-up table approximations, enable more sophisticated computations within the data-path. As a concrete example, we implement Timely by leveraging Tofino 2’s hardware timestamping to add TCP timestamps (<a href="#bib.bib83" class="ltx_ref">Borman et al., 2014</a>) and compute per-flow RTT exponentially weighted moving averages (EWMAs) in the data-path using its low-pass filter unit (<a href="#bib.bib89" class="ltx_ref">Gurevich and Fingerhut, 2021</a>). The control plane periodically adjusts transmission rates based on these RTTs, while the data-path enforces the rate limits.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Application co-design

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Databases (<a href="#bib.bib93" class="ltx_ref">Balakrishnan et al., 2020</a>), storage clusters (<a href="#bib.bib91" class="ltx_ref">Balakrishnan et al., 2013</a>), and replicated state machines (<a href="#bib.bib94" class="ltx_ref">Ding et al., 2020</a>) rely on shared logs to linearize state updates (<a href="#bib.bib90" class="ltx_ref">Luo et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib91" class="ltx_ref">Balakrishnan et al., 2013</a>). Inspired by NoPaxos (<a href="#bib.bib92" class="ltx_ref">Li et al., 2016</a>), we accelerate a shared log microbenchmark with a tailored Presto API: the application registers its log buffer and client connections, and Presto sequences incoming records across connections, appending them directly to the log via a buffer tail pointer within the data-path <span id="footnote2" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>2</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>2</sup> <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">2</span> Clients ensure log records are not fragmented across TCP segments.</span></span></span>. Our design guarantees that records from the same connection are appended to the shared log in order, resulting in a linearized shared log. If OOO segments arrive, Presto reserves space up to the highest sequence number, creating gaps that the application later fills from the corresponding segments Presto redirects to it.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.SSS1.Px4.p2" class="ltx_para">

We modify the Data Placement, Application Notification, and the libPresto API layers to accelerate a shared log microbenchmark in this way. With up to 32 clients (64–1024B records, 256 in-flight per client), Presto saturates line-rate with 1 core, versus 5 for TAS (3 TAS + 2 app). The gain comes from removing sequencing, protocol handling, and payload copying from the CPU in the common case (no loss). Similar RDMA acceleration is hard, especially for variable-sized records, since it needs multiple round-trips to sequence and write each record (<a href="#bib.bib95" class="ltx_ref">Kalia et al., 2016a</a>).

</div>

<figure id="S5.T2" class="ltx_table">
<table id="S5.T2.3" class="ltx_tabular ltx_align_middle">
<tbody class="ltx_tbody">
<tr id="S5.T2.3.1" class="ltx_tr">
<td id="S5.T2.3.1.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left"><span id="S5.T2.3.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">Extension</span></td>
<td id="S5.T2.3.1.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center"><span id="S5.T2.3.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">Blocks (Fig. <a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ Pipeline execution. ‣ 2.1. Match-Action Architecture ‣ 2. Background ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>)</span></td>
<td id="S5.T2.3.1.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right"><span id="S5.T2.3.1.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">P4 LoC</span></td>
<td id="S5.T2.3.1.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right"><span id="S5.T2.3.1.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:90%;">Power (W)</span></td>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 2. </span>Marginal cost of Presto extensions.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.4. </span>Robustness

<figure id="S5.F13" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S5.F13.fig1" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="S5.F13.fig2" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center">

</figure>
</div>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F13.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 13</span>. </span><span id="S5.F13.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Streaming goodput: (a) random drops, (b) incast.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Packet drops

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We measure the streaming throughput while randomly discarding packets with varying probability. This workload maintains many in-flight packets, ensuring losses trigger OOO processing. Due to recirculation bottlenecks (§<a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref" title="4. Implementation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>), we always use a TAS sender. This isolates the evaluation to receiver-side reassembly.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S5.F13" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 13 ‣ 5.4. Robustness ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">13</span></a>a shows that Presto sustains full throughput up to a $`0.1\%`$ drop rate, whereas TAS declines earlier—$`2\times`$ lower throughput at $`0.1\%`$ loss. Both provide 1-OOO reassembly on the receiver, but Presto’s faster acknowledgments accelerate recovery. Linux<span id="footnote3" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>3</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>3</sup> <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">3</span> Results for Linux and the Chelsio TOE can be found in (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>).</span></span></span> withstands higher loss rates via selective recovery (e.g., SACK), but cannot reach terabit throughput even without loss (<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Cai et al., 2021</a>), while Presto prioritizes fast, bounded-state hardware recovery. The ASIC-based Chelsio TOE degrades steeply even under moderate loss, and traditional RDMA drops all out-of-order segments, performing poorly under loss (<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">Mittal et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#bib.bib99" class="ltx_ref">Singhvi et al., 2025</a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Congestion control

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

To evaluate congestion control, we simulate incast by adding a traffic shaper frontend to a TAS receiver. The shaper limits ingress bandwidth (corresponding to the incast degree), uses tail-drop, and marks ECN above a queue occupancy threshold (determined experimentally as 130). Figure <a href="#S5.F13" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 13 ‣ 5.4. Robustness ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">13</span></a>b presents the results for a single-connection streaming workload under varying incast degrees. Black ticks mark the optimal bandwidth achievable at each degree.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

With its control-plane-driven DCTCP and credit-based rate enforcement, Presto effectively regulates transmission, minimizing loss and matching the shaped line rate. In contrast, Presto without congestion control (Presto-NoCC) transmits aggressively and causes excessive tail drops. Even when traffic shaping is disabled (incast degree=1), the Presto-NoCC sender significantly outpaces the TAS receiver, resulting in up to 50$`\times`$ lower throughput. TAS and Linux also regulate their rates effectively but fail to achieve maximum possible throughput at low incast degrees ($`\leq`$ 2) due to transport stack inefficiencies.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Large-scale simulation

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We next study large-scale behavior via simulation. Using TCT’s ns-3 (<a href="#bib.bib100" class="ltx_ref">Lim et al., 2021</a>) (TCT disabled), we test whether bounded OOO reassembly and selective recovery suffice for real datacenter loss patterns. We model a 96-node leaf-spine datacenter topology with latency-sensitive partition-aggregate incasts of 8KB queries competing with bandwidth-heavy background flows, drawn from real-world traces (<a href="#bib.bib100" class="ltx_ref">Lim et al., 2021</a>). We extend ns-3’s TCP to support varying reassembly fidelity, tracking up to $`N`$ OOO intervals. OOO-0 drops all OOO segments, while OOO-max tracks all segments with unlimited state.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px3.p2" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S5.T3" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 3 ‣ Congestion control ‣ 5.4. Robustness ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> compares the impact of different OOO fidelities. As expected, the OOO-0/go-back-N baseline performs poorly, greatly inflating tail FCTs. In contrast, Presto’s OOO-1 design with SACK substantially improves tail FCT, especially under DCTCP, which reacts proactively to congestion. Beyond 1–2 intervals, fidelity yields diminishing returns: congestion losses are typically bursty, so a few intervals suffice for efficient SACK-based recovery. Takeaway: selective recovery is compatible with Presto’s RMT pipeline, and a small, bounded OOO state (1–2 intervals) captures most of the benefit while remaining implementable on today’s hardware.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Performance isolation

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Run-to-completion TCP stacks struggle to isolate latency-sensitive (LS) and bandwidth-intensive (BI) connections. Specifically, TAS hashes connections to cores, mixing LS and BI connections, which causes interference, inflating LS tail latency and reducing BI bandwidth (<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Cai et al., 2021</a>).

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px4.p2" class="ltx_para">

To evaluate performance isolation, we co-locate an echo server RPC workload with 32 latency-sensitive (LS) connections (single in-flight request) and a streaming workload with 4 bandwidth-intensive (BI) connections (5 Gbps each). We first measure both in isolation, then compare with concurrent execution. We provision TAS with two fast-path cores to avoid bottlenecks.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.SSS0.Px4.p3" class="ltx_para">

As Figure <a href="#S5.F14" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 14 ‣ 5.5. Generalizability ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">14</span></a> shows, Presto delivers optimal performance for both LS and BI flows: consistent per-packet overhead and independent DMA channels steer payload directly to application cores, preventing cache pollution. Under co-location, TAS shows an 18% higher LS tail latency and 30% lower BI bandwidth.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS5" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.5. </span>Generalizability

<div id="S5.SS5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Prior FPGA work implements TCP as a monolithic circuit, demanding ever-shorter timing and more power to scale. Presto eases this with fine-grained per-state pipelining, even within a single connection. To investigate whether Presto generalizes to FPGA-based SmartNICs, we port Presto’s stateful egress pipeline to the AMD Alveo U250 (<a href="#bib.bib39" class="ltx_ref">AMD, 2026a</a>) using the VitisNet P4 compiler (<a href="#bib.bib103" class="ltx_ref">AMD, 2026b</a>). Our design meets 10ns timing for $`300`$ Mpps while using only $`2.5\%`$ of LUTs and $`0.78\%`$ of BRAM to support $`32`$K connections, consuming $`4.93`$W worst-case power. The low resource footprint leaves headroom to replicate the pipeline and shard connections for higher rates and connections.

</div>

<figure id="S5.F14" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F14.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 14</span>. </span><span id="S5.F14.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Performance isolation for connections.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS5.p2" class="ltx_para">

By comparison, on a similar FPGA with identical timing constraint, Tonic (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">Arashloo et al., 2020</a>) peaks at $`100`$ Mpps and consumes over $`20\%`$ of LUTs and BRAM, supporting only $`1`$K connections. Beehive (<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">Lim et al., 2024</a>) peaks at $`2.6`$ Mpps with comparable resource use. Direct comparison is limited since each design implements a different subset of TCP features—Tonic drops TCP’s byte-stream abstraction in favor of a fixed segment size, while Beehive omits OOO handling and congestion control but implements connection handshake.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">6. </span>Related Work

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Software stack improvements

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Software TCP stacks, including Linux and kernel-bypass designs (<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Kaufmann et al., 2019</a>; <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">Peter et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">Belay et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">Jeong et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#bib.bib62" class="ltx_ref">Zhang et al., 2021</a>), scale by distributing connections across threads with sequential per-connection processing (<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Cai et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Kaufmann et al., 2019</a>). Despite optimized interfaces (<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">Pesterev et al., 2012</a>; <a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">Lin et al., 2016</a>; <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">Jeong et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">Han et al., 2012</a>; <a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref">Linux, 2019</a>; <a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">Linux, 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">Documentation, 2025a</a>; <a href="#bib.bib61" class="ltx_ref">Yasukata et al., 2016</a>), drivers (<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref">Farshin et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">Flajslik and Rosenblum, 2013</a>; <a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref">Rizzo, 2012</a>; <a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref">Ghasemirahni et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#bib.bib84" class="ltx_ref">Pirelli and Candea, 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib85" class="ltx_ref">Schuh et al., 2024</a>), hardware assistance (<a href="#bib.bib59" class="ltx_ref">Documentation, 2025b</a>; <a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref">Intel, 2019</a>; <a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">Shinde et al., 2013</a>; <a href="#bib.bib60" class="ltx_ref">Documentation, 2025c</a>; <a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref">Intel, 2012</a>; <a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref">Intel, 2006</a>), and disaggregation (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">Cai et al., 2022</a>), these remain CPU-limited, failing to exploit network processing’s inherent packet-level parallelism. Presto instead pipelines TCP state updates across RMT stages even for a single connection, achieving line-rate processing with ASIC-class efficiency.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Co-designed transports

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Tightly coupled application-transport designs (<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">Kalia et al., 2019</a>; <a href="#bib.bib37" class="ltx_ref">Kalia et al., 2016b</a>; <a href="#bib.bib38" class="ltx_ref">Dragojević et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">Montazeri et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">Kogias et al., 2019</a>; <a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref">Hwang et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref">Singhvi et al., 2020</a>), including RDMA (<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">RDMA Consortium, 2009</a>), achieve high performance but burden developers with transport complexities, buffer management, and restrictive execution models, sacrificing generality and software reuse. Presto maintains broad TCP and POSIX compatibility, with flexibility for diverse application needs.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### FPGA stacks

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

FPGAs enable flexible offload (<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">Firestone et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">Putnam et al., 2016</a>), including for TCP. Tonic (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">Arashloo et al., 2020</a>) provides a sender-only TCP stack (evaluated in simulation), while other efforts (<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">Lim et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref">Ruiz et al., 2019</a>; <a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2020</a>) do not integrate with host applications (<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">Lim et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref">Ruiz et al., 2019</a>; <a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2020</a>). Partial offloads like ZeroNIC (<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">Skiadopoulos et al., 2024</a>) and Enso (<a href="#bib.bib63" class="ltx_ref">Sadok et al., 2023</a>) accelerate payload copies but leave TCP on the CPU.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p2" class="ltx_para">

These designs implement TCP state processing as monolithic *circuits* with tight per-packet timing (e.g., 10 ns in Tonic (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">Arashloo et al., 2020</a>)), making them hard to extend and power-intensive at high frequencies. Presto maps TCP to a pipelined match-action architecture that achieves (§<a href="#S5.SS5" class="ltx_ref" title="5.5. Generalizability ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.5</span></a>) higher throughput and lower resource usage than single-circuit designs, while retaining P4 agility over Verilog (<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">Putnam et al., 2016</a>; <a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">Sadok et al., 2021</a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### SmartNIC stacks

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

SmartNICs are widely used for network virtualization (<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref">Services, 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">Mehta, 2022</a>; <a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref">Sundar et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">Putnam et al., 2016</a>). AccelTCP (<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">Moon et al., 2020</a>) and IO-TCP (<a href="#bib.bib71" class="ltx_ref">Kim et al., 2023</a>) partially offload TCP; FlexTOE (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>) fully offloads the TCP data-path, but faces single-core NPU limits (<a href="#bib.bib86" class="ltx_ref">Chen et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>); Falcon (<a href="#bib.bib99" class="ltx_ref">Singhvi et al., 2025</a>) and SCR (<a href="#bib.bib98" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2025</a>) pair fixed transports with NPU congestion control. Data-parallel decompositions across NPU cores (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Marty et al., 2019</a>; <a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">Cai et al., 2022</a>; <a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>) are bounded by inter-core queueing, per-core variability, and serialized per-connection logic (FlexTOE’s *Protocol* module bottlenecks at $`\approx 8`$ Mpps/core (<a href="#bib.bib29" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara et al., 2022</a>)). This serialization is fundamental: even on a CPU, ZeroNIC (<a href="#bib.bib36" class="ltx_ref">Skiadopoulos et al., 2024</a>) reports a $`560`$ Gbps for single-core ceiling at 9K MTU. By pipelining TCP state updates across RMT, Presto extracts instruction-level parallelism, sustaining terabits within a single connection.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### RMT transport acceleration

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para">

RMT is emerging as a platform for flexible packet processing (<a href="#bib.bib77" class="ltx_ref">Li et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib78" class="ltx_ref">Lee et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref">Wang et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib80" class="ltx_ref">Kim et al., 2021</a>). NanoTransport (<a href="#bib.bib97" class="ltx_ref">Arslan et al., 2021</a>) uses P4 but offloads reassembly and packetization to FPGAs; Presto realizes both fully within RMT. R2P2 (<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">Kogias et al., 2019</a>) uses RMT for UDP-based RPC load balancing. GEM implements lightweight RDMA for external memory access (<a href="#bib.bib66" class="ltx_ref">Kim et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#bib.bib65" class="ltx_ref">Kim et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref">Scazzariello et al., 2023</a>). Conweave (<a href="#bib.bib68" class="ltx_ref">Song et al., 2023</a>) applies RMT to buffer out-of-order RDMA packets for network load balancing, and LinkGuardian (<a href="#bib.bib69" class="ltx_ref">Joshi et al., 2023</a>) adds link-local retransmissions. Other proposals leverage RMT for scheduling and congestion control (<a href="#bib.bib72" class="ltx_ref">Sharma et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#bib.bib73" class="ltx_ref">Sharma et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#bib.bib74" class="ltx_ref">Sharma et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#bib.bib75" class="ltx_ref">Goyal et al., 2020</a>). None provide full transport offload or obviate the need for an end-to-end reliable protocol like TCP, as Presto does.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">7. </span>Discussion

<div id="S7.p1" class="ltx_para">

We now turn to Presto’s limitations and its generalizability (<a href="#bib.bib105" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara, 2025</a>).

</div>

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Datacenter (DC) focus

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Presto targets DC environments—Bpps rates, Tbps bandwidth, $`\mu`$s latency, ASIC-efficiency, and software flexibility—exploiting their defining traits: persistent connections, congestion-driven (not corruption-driven) loss, short RPCs, and benign traffic patterns. Wide-area networks (WANs) and High-performance computing (HPC) settings may warrant different trade-offs, yet Presto’s principles still apply: BBR (<a href="#bib.bib106" class="ltx_ref">Cardwell et al., 2017</a>) is implementable, and although WAN loss rates may demand more OOO intervals, Presto’s OOO design may track in-flight segments efficiently for large bandwidth-delay-product (BDP) flows (<a href="#bib.bib107" class="ltx_ref">Lv et al., 2025</a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Advanced DC techniques

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Several techniques remain future work. Multi-pathing for AI cluster congestion (<a href="#bib.bib99" class="ltx_ref">Singhvi et al., 2025</a>; <a href="#bib.bib98" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2025</a>) maps onto Presto’s OOO intervals, which tracks cross-path reordering, though we do not evaluate it. RACK (<a href="#bib.bib104" class="ltx_ref">Cheng et al., 2021</a>), which tracks per-packet transmission times and scans $`O(\text{window})`$ unacknowledged packets, is harder given RMT’s strict state limits; but such settings run few large flows per host at modest packet rates, permitting slower RMT pipelines with stronger per-stage processing. Network stack disaggregation, enabled by Presto’s switch-based prototype, is also future work.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Message-boundary parsing

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Flexible offloads for higher-level protocols (e.g., RPC load balancing or access control) that inspect payloads need application-level message boundaries, which TCP’s byte-stream semantics obscure when delimiters straddle segments (§<a href="#S5.SS3.SSS1" class="ltx_ref" title="5.3.1. Case Studies ‣ 5.3. Flexibility ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.3.1</span></a>). Pushing message orientation into the transport solves this.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Head-of-Line (HoL) blocking with libPresto-ZC

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Presto’s zero-copy API transmits segments in allocation order, occasionally causing HoL delays. This rarely matters in practice: small RPCs can use copy-based APIs cheaply, and bulk flows can spread across connections. Presto can also expose queue-based interfaces (e.g., <span id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ibverbs</span>-style), via table-lookup address translation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Generalizability beyond TCP

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Presto’s principles extend beyond TCP to many transports (<a href="#bib.bib105" class="ltx_ref">Shashidhara, 2025</a>). As Tonic (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">Arashloo et al., 2020</a>) observes, reliable transports share common structural patterns: tracking in-flight segments for reassembly, recovering losses via retransmissions, and regulating in-flight segments to avoid congestion, differing only in mechanism. Presto realizes these efficiently on RMT architecture by overcoming the dependencies of Figure <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ Presto: A Match-Action TCP Stack for the Terabit Era"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S7.SS0.SSS0.Px5.p2" class="ltx_para">

Message-oriented transports (e.g., RDMA) are a case in point, replacing byte-stream reassembly with explicit message boundaries—no fundamental changes to Presto’s design. Messages are tracked by packet sequence number (PSN) rather than byte offsets, and OOO intervals map directly to PSN ranges. By decoupling data placement from application notification, Presto eliminates on-NIC buffering: each message either carries a host buffer address or draws from a queue of pre-allocated buffers (cf. RDMA WRITE vs. SEND), which Presto indexes to place directly into host memory, advancing the base index as the window moves. We leave implementation and evaluation to future work.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S8" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">8. </span>Conclusion

<div id="S8.p1" class="ltx_para">

Presto redefines the design-space of high-performance TCP stacks by showing that full transport functionality fits within RMT constraints. Embracing pipeline-parallelism, optimistic concurrency, and pseudo-segment updates, Presto delivers terabit throughput, low latency, and hardware efficiency while preserving software flexibility. Our Intel Tofino 2 prototype proves that RMT data planes can support stateful, loss-resilient, and scalable transport, laying a foundation for next-generation SmartNICs and datacenter networks.

</div>

<div id="S8.acknowledgements1" class="ltx_acknowledgements">

###### Acknowledgements.

We thank the anonymous reviewers and our shepherd for their helpful feedback. This work is supported in part by NSF grant 2212193 and the University of Washington Center for the Future of Cloud Infrastructure (FOCI). This work raises no ethical concerns.

</div>

</div>
